How to read this report
  • A run's score is the weighted fraction of its graders that passed; a "perfect" run passed every grader.
  • A case's score is the mean of its runs; a case passes when its score is at or above the 100% threshold (the tick on each case bar).
  • The suite score is the mean of the per-case scores.
  • Judge votes are independent samples of the LLM judge; the majority decides pass or fail.
  • "With plugin" and "Baseline" runs are identical except for the plugin being loaded; Δ is the with-plugin score minus the baseline score.

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The recovered model has an SNR of **30.3 dB** against the true model, and the script's last line is `SNR_DB=30.26244025911069`.

**What I chose and why**
- **Prior:** your model is made of flat blocks, so I used total-variation regularisation (penalising jumps between neighbouring samples, via `FirstDerivative`). It keeps sharp layer boundaries instead of smoothing them out.
- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman` with `mu=5`, 5 inner iterations (each up to 30 LSQR steps) and 200 outer iterations. The operator passes the dot-test.
- **Regularisation weight:** I picked it without looking at `xtrue`. The script tries 17 values of `epsRL1` from 0.1 to 10 and keeps the one whose data misfit is closest to the known noise level (σ = 0.02). That gave `epsRL1 = 0.75`, so it would work the same way on real data.

**How well it worked**

| Method | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Smooth L2 (second-derivative Tikhonov), for comparison | 13.5 dB |
| **TV split-Bregman (the script's result)** | **30.3 dB** |

- **Data fit:** the relative misfit is 0.0237 against a noise level of 0.0241, so the model fits the data to about the noise and no further.
- **Worst sample error:** 0.245, at the block edges where the blur has removed the most information.

**Convergence caveat:** the solver used all 200 outer iterations without meeting its stopping tolerance. The cost falls slowly (9.85 → 7.16), and between 200 and 400 iterations the model still changes by about 1%. I checked what happens as you run it longer:

| Outer iterations | SNR |
|---|---|
| 50 | 32.4 dB |
| 200 | 30.3 dB |
| 800 | 29.5 dB |

So the result settles around 29.5 dB, and stopping earlier acts as a little extra regularisation. I could get about 32 dB by choosing the iteration count or weight to match `xtrue`, but that uses the answer and wouldn't carry over to real data, so I didn't.

The script also saves `invert_results.npz` with the true model, data, TV and L2 results, cost history and chosen weights. If you want to tune further, jointly adjusting `mu`, `epsRL1` and the outer iteration count is the next step (the `tune-regularization` skill covers this).

"""Deblurring inversion of a 1-D blocky (layered) earth model with PyLops.

Prior: the model is piecewise constant -> Total-Variation (L1 on the first
derivative) regularisation solved with Split Bregman. The TV weight is chosen
with the discrepancy principle (known noise std sigma = 0.02), i.e. without
using the true model.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import splitbregman
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, n, n)

# ------------------------------------------------ baseline: smooth L2 (for reference)
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)
xl2 = regularized_inversion(Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[np.sqrt(0.1)], iter_lim=200)[0]
print(f"Baseline L2 smoothing (2nd derivative): SNR = {snr(xtrue, xl2):.2f} dB")

# ------------------------------------------------ TV via Split Bregman
D1 = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, kind="forward", edge=True)
mu = 5.0  # data-term weight; the effective TV strength is epsRL1 / mu
sb_kwargs = dict(niter_outer=200, niter_inner=5, tol=1e-8, tau=1.0,
                 iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10)


def tv_invert(eps):
    xinv, niter_out, cost = splitbregman(Op, y, [D1], mu=mu, epsRL1s=[eps],
                                         **sb_kwargs)
    return xinv, niter_out, cost


# Discrepancy principle: pick the eps whose data residual RMS is closest to sigma
epss = np.logspace(-1, 1, 17)
results = []
print("\n   epsRL1   resid_rms   (target sigma = %.3f)" % sigma)
for eps in epss:
    x, _, _ = tv_invert(eps)
    rms = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x) / np.sqrt(n)
    results.append((eps, rms))
    print(f"  {eps:7.3f}   {rms:9.4f}")
eps_best = min(results, key=lambda r: abs(r[1] - sigma))[0]

xinv, niter_out, cost = tv_invert(eps_best)
resid = y - Op @ xinv
print(f"\nChosen epsRL1 = {eps_best:.3f} (mu = {mu})")
print(f"Outer iterations used: {niter_out} / {sb_kwargs['niter_outer']}")
print(f"Cost: first = {cost[0]:.4e}, last = {cost[-1]:.4e}")
print(f"Relative data residual ||y-Op x||/||y|| = "
      f"{np.linalg.norm(resid) / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f} "
      f"(noise level {sigma * np.sqrt(n) / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f})")
print(f"Model error ||x - xtrue||_inf = {np.abs(xinv - xtrue).max():.3f}")

np.savez("invert_results.npz", xtrue=xtrue, y=y, xinv=xinv, xl2=xl2,
         cost=cost, eps=eps_best, mu=mu)

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
The inversion recovers the model at about **29.2 dB SNR** (last line of output: `SNR_DB=29.1957883247389`). `invert.py` is in the current folder and runs in a few seconds.

**What I chose and why**
- **Method:** total-variation regularisation (penalising jumps between neighbouring samples), solved with `pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman`. Your model is made of flat blocks, and this favours flat blocks with sharp edges. The penalty uses `FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="forward")` with `mu=1`, 300 outer × 5 inner iterations, and 30 lsqr iterations inside each inner step.
- **Regularisation weight:** I picked it with the discrepancy principle: the strongest weight whose data misfit stays below the expected noise size, σ·√N = 0.283. The true model is not used for this, so the method would work the same on real data. Of the weights tried (0.1 to 0.5), 0.35 was chosen, with a misfit of 0.279.
- **Checks:** the blur operator passes the adjoint dot-test. The solver ran all 300 outer iterations; over the last 10 the cost changed by only 5e-5 (relative), so it has converged. The relative data misfit is 0.0238 against a noise level of 0.0241, so it fits the data about as closely as the noise allows without fitting the noise.

**How well it worked**
| Approach | SNR vs true model |
|---|---|
| Smooth Tikhonov baseline (second derivative, ε = 0.1) | 13.9 dB |
| **Total variation, ε = 0.35 (final)** | **29.2 dB** |

The script also saves `xinv.npy` and `invert.png` (models, data/modelled/residual, and the cost curve).

**Caveats**
- Before writing the script, I ran a sweep that did compare against the true model. It showed that a slightly weaker weight (ε ≈ 0.2–0.3) reaches about 30.1 dB. I didn't use that, because on real data you have no true model to tune against. The gap is under 1 dB.
- Total variation slightly lowers the height of narrow blocks. If you need exact amplitudes, the next step would be an edge-preserving method that corrects for this, which needs PyProximal (not installed here). Fine-tuning ε further is what the `tune-regularization` skill covers.

"""Deblurring of a 1-D blocky (layered) model with PyLops.

Solver: split Bregman with an L1 penalty on the first derivative (anisotropic
total variation), which favours piecewise-constant models:

    min_x  mu/2 ||y - Op x||_2^2 + eps ||D x||_1

The TV weight eps is chosen with the discrepancy principle (data residual
~ sigma * sqrt(N)), i.e. without using the true model.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

pylops.utils.dottest(Op, n, n, raiseerror=True)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- inversion
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="forward")
mu = 1.0
niter_outer, niter_inner = 300, 5


def tv_inversion(eps):
    xinv, itn, cost = pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D], niter_outer=niter_outer, niter_inner=niter_inner,
        mu=mu, epsRL1s=[eps], tol=1e-8, tau=1.0,
        iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10,  # inner lsqr
    )
    return xinv, itn, cost


# discrepancy principle: pick the largest eps whose residual stays within
# the expected noise norm
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)
eps_grid = [0.1, 0.2, 0.25, 0.3, 0.35, 0.4, 0.5]
print(f"target residual sigma*sqrt(N) = {target:.4f}")
results = {}
for eps in eps_grid:
    x, itn, cost = tv_inversion(eps)
    res = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x)
    results[eps] = (x, itn, cost, res)
    print(f"eps={eps:<5} residual={res:.4f}")
admissible = [e for e in eps_grid if results[e][3] <= target]
eps = max(admissible) if admissible else min(eps_grid)
xinv, itn, cost, res = results[eps]

# baseline: smooth (second-derivative Tikhonov) inversion for comparison
xl2 = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [pylops.SecondDerivative(n)], epsRs=[0.1], iter_lim=200)[0]

print(f"chosen eps={eps}, outer iterations={itn}, "
      f"final cost={cost[-1]:.4e} (relative change last 10 its: "
      f"{abs(cost[-1] - cost[-11]) / cost[-1]:.1e})")
print(f"relative data residual ||y-Op x||/||y|| = {res / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f} "
      f"(noise level {target / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f})")
print(f"SNR Tikhonov (2nd derivative) baseline = {snr(xtrue, xl2):.2f} dB")
np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)

try:
    import matplotlib
    matplotlib.use("Agg")
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(14, 3.5))
    axs[0].plot(xtrue, "k", lw=2, label="true")
    axs[0].plot(xl2, "tab:orange", label="L2 smooth")
    axs[0].plot(xinv, "tab:blue", label=f"TV (eps={eps})")
    axs[0].set_title("Model"); axs[0].legend()
    axs[1].plot(y, "k.", ms=3, label="data")
    axs[1].plot(Op @ xinv, "tab:blue", label="modelled")
    axs[1].plot(y - Op @ xinv, "tab:red", label="residual")
    axs[1].set_title("Data"); axs[1].legend()
    axs[2].semilogy(cost); axs[2].set_title("Split Bregman cost")
    axs[2].set_xlabel("outer iteration")
    fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("invert.png", dpi=120)
except ImportError:
    pass

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The recovered model has an SNR of **29.2 dB** against the true model, and the script's last line is `SNR_DB=29.195788324738942`.

**What I chose and why**
- **Operator check:** `Convolve1D` passes the dot-test, so its forward and adjoint agree.
- **Regularisation: total variation.** Your model is made of flat layers with sharp jumps, so I penalised the sum of absolute sample-to-sample changes (L1 norm of `FirstDerivative`). That keeps the steps sharp instead of smearing them out.
- **Solver: `splitbregman`.** This is PyLops's solver for that kind of penalty. I set `mu=1`, 300 outer × 5 inner iterations, and 30 LSQR iterations per inner step. Larger `mu` values in my trials gave erratic results that depended on when the run stopped.
- **Picking the regularisation weight λ:** I used the discrepancy principle. The script tries λ from 0.05 to 0.60 and keeps the largest λ whose data residual RMS stays at or below the noise level σ = 0.02. That selects **λ = 0.35** (residual RMS 0.0197). The true model isn't used for this choice, only for the final SNR, so the same approach works on real data if you know the noise level.

**How well it worked**

| Method | SNR vs true model |
|---|---|
| LSQR, no regularisation (50 iterations) | 11.0 dB |
| Tikhonov, smoothing on 1st derivative (ε = 0.03) | 13.8 dB |
| **Total variation, Split-Bregman (λ = 0.35)** | **29.2 dB** |

- **Convergence:** the cost changed by only 3e-4 (relative) over the last 50 iterations, so the result is essentially converged.
- **Data fit:** the residual matches the noise level, so the inversion isn't fitting the noise.
- **Headroom:** in exploratory sweeps, using the true model to pick the best λ scored at most about 30.1 dB (λ ≈ 0.2–0.3). The noise-based choice is within 1 dB of that.

**What's left:** the remaining error is mostly slight rounding and small amplitude loss at the layer edges, which the blur and noise make impossible to recover fully. If you ever don't know σ, use an L-curve to choose λ instead.

Files are in this folder:
- `invert.py`
- `invert_results.npz` (all the models and the cost curve)
- `invert.png` (plot of the models and the data fit)

"""TV-regularised deconvolution of a 1-D blocky layered model with PyLops.

The model is piecewise constant, so we invert with total variation
(L1 norm of the first derivative) via Split-Bregman. The TV weight is
chosen with the discrepancy principle (data residual RMS ~ noise sigma),
which does not use the true model; xtrue is only used to report the SNR.
"""
import warnings

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

warnings.filterwarnings("ignore")

# ------------------------------------------------------------------ synthetic
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, n, n)

# ------------------------------------------------------------------ baselines
xlsqr = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, niter=50)[0]
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="backward")
xtik = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D], epsRs=[0.03], iter_lim=200
)[0]


# ------------------------------------------------------------ TV inversion
def tv_invert(lam, mu=1.0, niter_outer=300):
    """Split-Bregman: min 1/2||y - Op x||^2 + lam ||D x||_1 (mu = 1)."""
    xinv, iiter, cost = pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D],
        niter_outer=niter_outer, niter_inner=5, mu=mu, epsRL1s=[lam * mu],
        tol=1e-8, tau=1.0, iter_lim=30, damp=0,
    )
    return xinv, iiter, cost


# Discrepancy principle: largest lam whose residual RMS does not exceed sigma
lams = np.arange(0.05, 0.61, 0.05)
print(" lam   iters  rms_res   (target rms = sigma = %.3f)" % sigma)
best = None
for lam in lams:
    xl, it, _ = tv_invert(lam)
    rms = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ xl) / np.sqrt(n)
    print(f"{lam:5.2f}  {it:5d}  {rms:.4f}")
    if rms <= sigma:
        best = (lam, xl, it, rms)
lam, xinv, iiter, rms = best

# convergence check: cost change over the last iterations
_, _, cost = tv_invert(lam)
print(f"\nChosen lam = {lam:.2f}, outer iterations = {iiter}, "
      f"residual RMS = {rms:.4f} (sigma = {sigma})")
print(f"Relative cost change over last 50 its: "
      f"{abs(cost[-1] - cost[-51]) / cost[-1]:.2e}")
print(f"Relative data residual ||y-Op x||/||y|| = "
      f"{np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ xinv) / np.linalg.norm(y):.4f}")

print(f"\nSNR lsqr (50 its, no reg)     : {snr(xtrue, xlsqr):6.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR Tikhonov 1st derivative   : {snr(xtrue, xtik):6.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR TV Split-Bregman          : {snr(xtrue, xinv):6.2f} dB")

np.savez("invert_results.npz", xtrue=xtrue, y=y, xinv=xinv,
         xlsqr=xlsqr, xtik=xtik, cost=cost, lam=lam)

try:
    import matplotlib
    matplotlib.use("Agg")
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

    fig, axs = plt.subplots(2, 1, figsize=(9, 6), sharex=True)
    axs[0].plot(xtrue, "k", lw=3, label="true")
    axs[0].plot(xtik, "tab:blue", label="Tikhonov")
    axs[0].plot(xinv, "tab:red", lw=1.5, label=f"TV (lam={lam:.2f})")
    axs[0].set_title("Model"); axs[0].legend()
    axs[1].plot(y, "k.", ms=3, label="data")
    axs[1].plot(Op @ xinv, "tab:red", label="modelled (TV)")
    axs[1].plot(y - Op @ xinv, "tab:gray", label="residual")
    axs[1].set_title("Data"); axs[1].legend()
    fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("invert.png", dpi=120)
except ImportError:
    pass

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The recovered model has an SNR of **29.2 dB** against the true model, and the script's last line is `SNR_DB=29.195788324738942`.

**What I chose and why**
- **Operator check:** `Convolve1D` passes the dot-test, so its forward and adjoint agree.
- **Regularisation: total variation.** Your model is made of flat layers with sharp jumps, so I penalised the sum of absolute sample-to-sample changes (L1 norm of `FirstDerivative`). That keeps the steps sharp instead of smearing them out.
- **Solver: `splitbregman`.** This is PyLops's solver for that kind of penalty. I set `mu=1`, 300 outer × 5 inner iterations, and 30 LSQR iterations per inner step. Larger `mu` values in my trials gave erratic results that depended on when the run stopped.
- **Picking the regularisation weight λ:** I used the discrepancy principle. The script tries λ from 0.05 to 0.60 and keeps the largest λ whose data residual RMS stays at or below the noise level σ = 0.02. That selects **λ = 0.35** (residual RMS 0.0197). The true model isn't used for this choice, only for the final SNR, so the same approach works on real data if you know the noise level.

**How well it worked**

| Method | SNR vs true model |
|---|---|
| LSQR, no regularisation (50 iterations) | 11.0 dB |
| Tikhonov, smoothing on 1st derivative (ε = 0.03) | 13.8 dB |
| **Total variation, Split-Bregman (λ = 0.35)** | **29.2 dB** |

- **Convergence:** the cost changed by only 3e-4 (relative) over the last 50 iterations, so the result is essentially converged.
- **Data fit:** the residual matches the noise level, so the inversion isn't fitting the noise.
- **Headroom:** in exploratory sweeps, using the true model to pick the best λ scored at most about 30.1 dB (λ ≈ 0.2–0.3). The noise-based choice is within 1 dB of that.

**What's left:** the remaining error is mostly slight rounding and small amplitude loss at the layer edges, which the blur and noise make impossible to recover fully. If you ever don't know σ, use an L-curve to choose λ instead.

Files are in this folder:
- `invert.py`
- `invert_results.npz` (all the models and the cost curve)
- `invert.png` (plot of the models and the data fit)

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. It takes about 9 s, and the last line it prints is **`SNR_DB=29.8065`**.

**What I chose**
- **Regularisation: total variation.** The true model is flat blocks with sharp jumps, so I penalised the absolute size of the changes between neighbouring samples (L1 norm of the first derivative). That favours blocky solutions with sharp edges. I solved it with PyLops' Split-Bregman solver (`pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman`) with `mu=10`, 1000 outer and 5 inner iterations.
- **Picking the regularisation strength without using the true model.** The script tries TV weights from 0.02 to 0.15 and keeps the strongest one whose data misfit ‖y − Op x‖ stays at or below the expected noise level, σ√n ≈ 0.283. This is the discrepancy principle. It picked a weight of 0.1 (misfit 0.274).
- **Baseline for comparison.** A smooth L2 (Tikhonov) inversion on the first derivative only reaches **13.7 dB**. It rounds off the block edges.

**How well it worked**
- The TV inversion reaches **29.8 dB**, about 16 dB better than the L2 baseline.
- Before writing the script I scanned the parameters by hand, looking at the true model. The best result there was about 31 dB, with a weight of about 0.08 and fewer iterations. So choosing by noise level lands within about 1 dB of the best case without looking at the answer.
- The method relies on knowing the noise level (σ = 0.02). That's fine for this synthetic. With real data you'd need to estimate σ, or pick the weight another way, such as with an L-curve.
- A larger `mu`, like 100, converged much more slowly with this setup, which is why I kept `mu=10`.

"""TV-regularised deconvolution of a blocky 1-D model with PyLops.

The model is piecewise constant, so we use a total-variation (L1 on the first
derivative) regulariser solved with Split-Bregman. The regularisation weight is
chosen by the discrepancy principle (data residual ~ noise level), i.e. without
looking at the true model.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---- synthetic -------------------------------------------------------------
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# ---- baseline: smooth (L2 on derivative) Tikhonov ---------------------------
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="forward")
xl2 = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D], epsRs=[0.1], iter_lim=500
)[0]
print(f"L2 (Tikhonov, eps=0.1) baseline: SNR = {snr(xtrue, xl2):.2f} dB")

# ---- TV inversion via Split-Bregman ----------------------------------------
# Objective: mu/2 ||y - Op x||^2 + lam ||D x||_1  -> effective TV weight = lam/mu
mu = 10.0
target = sigma * np.sqrt(n)  # expected residual norm for white noise


def tv_invert(weight):
    return pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D], niter_outer=1000, niter_inner=5, epsRL1s=[weight * mu],
        mu=mu, tau=1.0, tol=1e-12, x0=np.zeros(n), iter_lim=50, damp=1e-10,
    )[0]


# Discrepancy principle: largest TV weight whose residual stays <= target
weights = [0.02, 0.04, 0.06, 0.08, 0.1, 0.12, 0.15]
best_w, xinv = None, None
print(f"Target residual norm (sigma*sqrt(n)) = {target:.3f}")
for w in weights:
    x = tv_invert(w)
    res = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x)
    print(f"  TV weight {w:5.2f}: residual = {res:.3f}")
    if res <= target:
        best_w, xinv = w, x
if xinv is None:  # fall back to the least-regularised solution
    best_w = weights[0]
    xinv = tv_invert(best_w)

print(f"Chosen TV weight = {best_w} (mu={mu})")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv):.4f}")

"""1-D deblurring of a blocky layered-earth model with PyLops.

Since the true model is piecewise constant, we use total-variation (TV)
regularisation, i.e. an L1 penalty on the first derivative of the model:

    min_x  1/2 ||y - Op x||_2^2  +  lam ||D x||_1

solved with Split Bregman. The TV weight ``lam`` is picked with the
discrepancy principle (RMS data residual ~ noise std), which uses only the
data and the noise level and never looks at the true model. A smooth
Tikhonov (L2 on D x) solution is also computed for comparison.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# Synthetic setup
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, kind="forward", edge=True)

# Baseline: Tikhonov (smoothness) regularisation
xtik = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D], epsRs=[0.1], iter_lim=500
)[0]
print(f"Tikhonov (eps=0.1)       SNR = {snr(xtrue, xtik):6.2f} dB")


# TV regularisation via Split Bregman
def tv_inversion(lam, mu=10.0):
    return pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D], niter_outer=100, niter_inner=5, mu=mu, epsRL1s=[lam],
        tol=1e-6, tau=1.0, iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10,
    )[0]


# Discrepancy principle: choose the largest lam whose RMS residual stays
# within ~5% of the noise level
lams = [0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0, 1.25, 1.5, 2.0, 3.0]
best = None
for lam in lams:
    x = tv_inversion(lam)
    res = np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ x) / np.sqrt(n)
    print(f"TV lam={lam:4.2f}  residual RMS = {res:.4f}")
    if res <= 1.05 * sigma:
        best = (lam, x)
lam, xinv = best
print(f"Selected lam = {lam} (discrepancy principle, sigma = {sigma})")
print(f"TV (Split Bregman)       SNR = {snr(xtrue, xinv):6.2f} dB")

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote and ran `invert.py`. It recovers the model at **29.0 dB SNR** and the last line it prints is `SNR_DB=28.958459708147828`. It takes about 9 seconds.

**What I chose**
- **Total-variation regularisation.** Your model is blocky: flat layers with sharp jumps. A TV penalty (the L1 norm of the first derivative, via `pylops.FirstDerivative`) favours exactly that. Smoothing penalties blur the jumps instead.
- **Solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman` with `mu = 1`, 200 outer and 5 inner iterations, and LSQR with 30 iterations for each inner solve.
- **Regularisation strength:** chosen automatically by the discrepancy principle. The script bisects on the TV weight ε until the data residual RMS equals the noise level σ = 0.02. That gave ε ≈ 0.368. This rule uses only the noise level, never `xtrue`, so it's a fair choice on real data where σ is known or can be estimated.

**How well it worked**

| Method | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Plain LSQR (no regularisation, 50 iterations) | 11.0 dB |
| Tikhonov, second-derivative smoothing (ε = 0.1) | 13.9 dB |
| **TV with Split-Bregman (discrepancy ε)** | **29.0 dB** |

In a manual scan with ε = 0.05 to 1.0, the error against the truth was smallest around ε ≈ 0.3–0.4, peaking at about 30.6 dB at ε = 0.3. The automatic rule landed in that range without looking at the true model. Smaller ε overfit the noise, and larger ε flattened the layers.

**Things to keep in mind**
- The script needs σ. With real data you'd estimate it, for example from a quiet part of the trace.
- If the noise is misjudged, ε will shift.
- TV slightly reduces the step heights. With a known noise level this is close to the best this approach can do.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.5

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""Deblurring inversion of a 1-D blocky (layered) model with PyLops.

The model is piecewise constant, so we use total-variation (TV) regularisation
solved with Split-Bregman. The TV weight is picked by the discrepancy
principle: the data residual RMS should match the known noise level sigma.
This uses only the noise level, never the true model.
"""
import warnings

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

warnings.filterwarnings("ignore")

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic
n = 200
xtrue = np.zeros(n)
xtrue[30:70] = 1.0
xtrue[70:110] = -0.5
xtrue[140:170] = 2.0

t = np.arange(-20, 21)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 5) ** 2)
h /= h.sum()
Op = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(n, h=h, offset=20)

sigma = 0.02
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
y = Op @ xtrue + sigma * rng.standard_normal(n)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- inversion
D = pylops.FirstDerivative(n, edge=True, kind="forward")
mu = 1.0


def tv_inversion(eps):
    return pylops.optimization.sparsity.splitbregman(
        Op, y, [D],
        niter_outer=200, niter_inner=5, mu=mu, epsRL1s=[eps],
        tol=1e-8, tau=1.0, x0=np.zeros(n),
        **dict(iter_lim=30, damp=1e-10),
    )[0]


def residual_rms(x):
    return np.linalg.norm(Op @ x - y) / np.sqrt(n)


# Discrepancy principle: bisect (in log-space) on eps so residual RMS = sigma.
# Residual grows monotonically with eps.
lo, hi = 1e-2, 1e1
for _ in range(20):
    eps = np.sqrt(lo * hi)
    if residual_rms(tv_inversion(eps)) < sigma:
        lo = eps
    else:
        hi = eps
eps = np.sqrt(lo * hi)
xinv = tv_inversion(eps)

# Baselines for comparison
xlsqr = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, np.zeros(n), niter=50)[0]
D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(n, edge=True)
xtik = pylops.optimization.leastsquares.regularized_inversion(
    Op, y, [D2], epsRs=[0.1], **dict(iter_lim=500)
)[0]

print(f"Noise sigma           : {sigma}")
print(f"Chosen TV eps (mu={mu}) : {eps:.4f}")
print(f"Residual RMS          : {residual_rms(xinv):.4f}")
print(f"SNR LSQR (no reg)     : {snr(xtrue, xlsqr):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR Tikhonov (D2)     : {snr(xtrue, xtik):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR TV Split-Bregman  : {snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")
✓ pass prior-choice

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The robust inversion recovers the model at about **60 dB SNR**, while an ordinary least-squares fit gets only 2.5 dB.

**What I chose and why:** the odd readings are a few very large spikes (outliers) on top of small noise. A normal least-squares fit tries to match those spikes too, and they pull the whole answer off. So I used `pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls` with `kind="data"`. Each round, it reduces the weight of readings that fit badly, which comes close to a fit that ignores extreme values. The problem has 300 readings for 50 unknowns and the operator passes the dot-test, so it needs no extra regularisation. The inversion uses only `A` and `y`; `xtrue` is used only to compute the SNR at the end.

**Results:**

| | SNR vs. true model |
|---|---|
| `lsqr` (plain least squares, for comparison) | 2.49 dB |
| `irls` (robust fit) | **60.34 dB** |

- **Noise level:** estimated from the robust fit's residuals, the noise is σ ≈ 0.0095, which matches the 0.01 in your synthetic data. On the readings that weren't flagged, the leftover error is 0.0101, so the fit matches the data to the noise level without over-fitting.
- **Bad readings:** readings with errors above 5σ are flagged as outliers. That flags 30 readings, the same number you corrupted. I only compared the count, not which readings they were. In the real survey this flag list shows you which sensors to check.
- **Convergence:** IRLS ran all 50 of its rounds (`nouter=50`) rather than stopping early at `tolIRLS`. The SNR shows the result is fine anyway.
- **Output:** the recovered model is saved to `xinv.npy`, and the last printed line is `SNR_DB=60.33720477825759`.

**Settings to tune if you need to:** `nouter` (number of rounds) and `epsR` (a small value that keeps the reweighting stable) in the `irls` call. For real data, check that the flagged readings are plausible sensor faults. If far more than about 10% of readings turn out bad, or the bad values are not large, these settings may need retuning.

"""Robust inversion of sensor data contaminated by outliers.

Only `A` and `y` are used by the inversion; `xtrue` is used solely to report SNR.
Solver: IRLS with an L1 data misfit (kind="data"), which down-weights readings
with large residuals, so the few corrupted sensors do not bias the model.
A plain L2 least-squares (lsqr) solution is shown for comparison.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# --- synthetic data (in the real survey only A and y are available) ---
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# --- inversion: uses only A and y from here on ---
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, *Op.shape)

# Baseline: ordinary L2 least squares
xl2, istop, itn = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), niter=100, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10)[:3]
print(f"LSQR (L2):   istop={istop} iters={itn} SNR={snr(xtrue, xl2):.2f} dB")

# Robust: IRLS approximating an L1 data misfit
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), nouter=50, epsR=1e-8, epsI=0.0, tolIRLS=1e-8,
    kind="data", iter_lim=100,  # inner scipy lsqr iterations
)
res = y - Op @ xinv

# Flag outliers from the robust residual (MAD-based scale estimate)
sigma = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(res - np.median(res)))
flagged = np.abs(res) > 5 * sigma
print(f"IRLS (L1):   outer iters={nouter}")
print(f"Robust noise estimate sigma={sigma:.4f}; flagged {flagged.sum()} outlier readings")
print(f"Residual RMS on non-flagged readings: {np.sqrt(np.mean(res[~flagged] ** 2)):.4f}")

np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py`, ran it, and it recovers `x` well: **SNR ≈ 60.5 dB**, compared with 2.5 dB for ordinary least squares.

**What I chose and why**
- **The odd readings are outliers.** About 10% of the readings (30 of 300) are off by 20 to 50 units, while the noise is only about 0.01. A standard least-squares fit (L2 misfit) gets pulled toward every one of those spikes, and the model comes out almost useless.
- **Robust solver: IRLS with an L1 data misfit.** I used `pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls` with `kind="data"`. It repeatedly lowers the weight of readings with large residuals, which is close to minimising the sum of absolute residuals, so a few big spikes carry little weight.
- **No model regularisation.** With 300 readings and 50 unknowns the problem is well determined, so there's nothing extra to tune.
- **Only `y` and `A` go into the inversion.** The script builds the synthetic data exactly as in your snippet, but the solver never sees `bad` or `xtrue`. `xtrue` is used only for the final `SNR_DB` line.

**Results**

| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Least squares (LSQR, baseline) | 2.49 dB |
| **IRLS, L1 misfit** | **60.47 dB** |

- **Noise level matches.** The typical size of the leftover residuals, estimated robustly (MAD), is 0.0097, which matches the true noise of 0.01. So the fit isn't overfitting or underfitting the good readings.
- **Outlier flagging.** The script flags readings whose residual is more than 5 times that noise estimate. It flagged exactly 30, and a separate check showed these are exactly the 30 corrupted indices. In the real survey this list tells you which sensors to look at.
- **Iterations.** It used all 50 outer iterations, and the answer was already at the noise floor.
- **Last line printed:** `SNR_DB=60.46647045939035`

**Outputs:** `xinv.npy` (the inverted model) and `outliers.npy` (indices of the flagged readings).

**Worth knowing**
- **Keyword fix.** In this PyLops install the inner solver is scipy's `lsqr`, so the keyword for its iteration limit is `iter_lim`, not `maxiter` as the docstring says. The script uses `iter_lim`.
- **`epsR=1e-8`** keeps readings at the noise level from being down-weighted, and should stay well below your real noise level. If real data behave less cleanly, one option is to drop the flagged readings and solve least squares on the rest.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""Robust inversion of sensor data containing outliers with PyLops.

Solver choice: the data contain a handful of grossly wrong readings (spikes of
tens of units on top of ~0.01 noise). A least-squares (L2) misfit lets every
spike pull the solution, so we minimise an L1 data misfit instead, using IRLS
(`pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls`, kind="data"). The problem is well
overdetermined (300 x 50), so no model regularisation is needed. Only `y` and
`A` are used by the inversion; `xtrue` is used solely for the final SNR.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic data
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- inversion
# From here on, only `A` (via Op) and `y` are used.
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)
assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, *Op.shape)

# Baseline: plain least squares (L2 misfit), for comparison only
xls, istop, itn_ls = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), niter=100, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10)[:3]

# Robust: IRLS with L1 data misfit. epsR sets the residual level below which
# a sample is no longer down-weighted; keep it well below the noise level.
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), nouter=50, kind="data",
    epsR=1e-8, epsI=1e-10, tolIRLS=1e-8, warm=True,
    iter_lim=200,  # inner scipy lsqr iterations on the weighted problem
)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- diagnostics
r = y - Op @ xinv
mad = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r - np.median(r)))  # robust noise estimate
flagged = np.flatnonzero(np.abs(r) > 5 * mad)

print(f"LSQR (L2) : istop={istop}, iters={itn_ls}, "
      f"SNR={snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")
print(f"IRLS (L1) : outer iters={nouter}")
print(f"Robust residual std (MAD) = {mad:.4f}")
print(f"Flagged {flagged.size} readings as outliers (|r| > 5 MAD)")
print(f"Relative residual on inliers = "
      f"{np.linalg.norm(np.delete(r, flagged)) / np.linalg.norm(np.delete(y, flagged)):.2e}")
np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
np.save("outliers.npy", flagged)

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py`, ran it, and it recovers `x` well: **SNR ≈ 60.5 dB**, compared with 2.5 dB for ordinary least squares.

**What I chose and why**
- **The odd readings are outliers.** About 10% of the readings (30 of 300) are off by 20 to 50 units, while the noise is only about 0.01. A standard least-squares fit (L2 misfit) gets pulled toward every one of those spikes, and the model comes out almost useless.
- **Robust solver: IRLS with an L1 data misfit.** I used `pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls` with `kind="data"`. It repeatedly lowers the weight of readings with large residuals, which is close to minimising the sum of absolute residuals, so a few big spikes carry little weight.
- **No model regularisation.** With 300 readings and 50 unknowns the problem is well determined, so there's nothing extra to tune.
- **Only `y` and `A` go into the inversion.** The script builds the synthetic data exactly as in your snippet, but the solver never sees `bad` or `xtrue`. `xtrue` is used only for the final `SNR_DB` line.

**Results**

| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Least squares (LSQR, baseline) | 2.49 dB |
| **IRLS, L1 misfit** | **60.47 dB** |

- **Noise level matches.** The typical size of the leftover residuals, estimated robustly (MAD), is 0.0097, which matches the true noise of 0.01. So the fit isn't overfitting or underfitting the good readings.
- **Outlier flagging.** The script flags readings whose residual is more than 5 times that noise estimate. It flagged exactly 30, and a separate check showed these are exactly the 30 corrupted indices. In the real survey this list tells you which sensors to look at.
- **Iterations.** It used all 50 outer iterations, and the answer was already at the noise floor.
- **Last line printed:** `SNR_DB=60.46647045939035`

**Outputs:** `xinv.npy` (the inverted model) and `outliers.npy` (indices of the flagged readings).

**Worth knowing**
- **Keyword fix.** In this PyLops install the inner solver is scipy's `lsqr`, so the keyword for its iteration limit is `iter_lim`, not `maxiter` as the docstring says. The script uses `iter_lim`.
- **`epsR=1e-8`** keeps readings at the noise level from being down-weighted, and should stay well below your real noise level. If real data behave less cleanly, one option is to drop the flagged readings and solve least squares on the rest.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The robust inversion recovers `x` with an SNR of about **60.5 dB**, against 2.5 dB for ordinary least squares.

**What I chose and why**
- **The problem:** `A` is 300×50, so there are more readings than unknowns, and it's well conditioned (cond ≈ 2.3). The PyLops adjoint check (dot-test) passes. The difficulty isn't the operator; it's the odd readings. A few large spikes dominate a squared-error fit and pull the whole model off.
- **The solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls` with `kind="data"`. It minimises the sum of absolute residuals instead of their squares, by solving a series of weighted least-squares problems. Each round, readings with large residuals get less weight, so the spikes are ignored and you don't need to know in advance which readings are bad. Settings: 50 outer iterations, up to 100 inner scipy `lsqr` iterations, `epsR=1e-8` (keeps the weights stable for near-zero residuals), and no extra damping on the model.
- **Inputs:** the inversion uses only `y` and `A`. `xtrue` appears only in the final SNR line.

**Results**

| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Plain `lsqr` (squared error) | 2.49 dB |
| IRLS (absolute error) | **60.48 dB** |

- **Residuals on the good readings:** after the robust fit, their typical size is about 0.0100 (robust estimate 0.0097), which matches the 0.01 noise you added. So the fit is neither overfitting nor underfitting the clean data.
- **Outlier flagging:** the script flags readings whose residual is more than 5× that typical size. It flags exactly 30. In a separate check using the generator's hidden indices, those were exactly the 30 corrupted ones. The flags are saved to `outlier_mask.npy` and the model to `xinv.npy`.
- **Relative residual:** `||y − Ax|| / ||y||` is 0.86 for IRLS. That's expected, because the outliers are deliberately left unfitted.
- **Iterations:** IRLS used all 50 outer iterations without meeting its very tight stopping tolerance (1e-10). The result had clearly settled well before that, so this doesn't matter in practice.

The last line printed is `SNR_DB=60.48386318547998`.

**For your real survey:** the 5× threshold and `epsR` are the settings to revisit. If you know roughly how noisy the good sensors are, set `epsR` well below that noise level squared. If the good readings also have heavy-tailed noise, not just a few gross errors, you can reweight a second time using only the unflagged readings.

"""Robust inversion of sensor data containing outliers with PyLops.

Only ``y`` and ``A`` are used by the inversion; ``xtrue`` is used solely to
report the quality metric on this synthetic example.
"""

import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic data
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- sanity checks
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A, dtype="float64")
assert pylops.utils.dottest(Op, *Op.shape)
print(f"Op shape: {Op.shape} (overdetermined), cond(A) = {np.linalg.cond(A):.2f}")

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- L2 baseline
xl2, istop, itn, r1norm = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), niter=100, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10)[:4]
print(f"\nLSQR (L2 misfit): istop={istop}, iters={itn}, "
      f"||y-Ax||/||y||={np.linalg.norm(y - Op @ xl2) / np.linalg.norm(y):.3e}, "
      f"SNR={snr(xtrue, xl2):.2f} dB")

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- robust L1 misfit (IRLS)
# Minimises ||y - Op x||_1 by iteratively reweighted least squares: residuals
# of large amplitude (outliers) get small weights at every outer iteration.
# epsR stabilises weights of near-zero residuals; set well below the noise
# power (noise std ~1e-2 -> 1e-4) so good data keep nearly-L1 weighting.
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), nouter=50, threshR=False, epsR=1e-8, epsI=0.0,
    tolIRLS=1e-10, warm=True, kind="data", iter_lim=100, atol=1e-10, btol=1e-10,
)
res = y - Op @ xinv
print(f"\nIRLS (L1 misfit): outer iters={nouter}, "
      f"||y-Ax||/||y||={np.linalg.norm(res) / np.linalg.norm(y):.3e}")

# Residual-based outlier flagging (robust sigma via MAD) - uses only y and A
sigma = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(res - np.median(res)))
flagged = np.abs(res) > 5 * sigma
good_rms = np.sqrt(np.mean(res[~flagged] ** 2))
print(f"robust residual sigma = {sigma:.3e}, RMS residual on unflagged data = {good_rms:.3e}")
print(f"flagged {flagged.sum()} readings as outliers")

np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
np.save("outlier_mask.npy", flagged)

print(f"\nSNR L2 baseline = {snr(xtrue, xl2):.2f} dB, SNR IRLS = {snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The robust inversion recovers `x` with an SNR of about **60.5 dB**, against 2.5 dB for ordinary least squares.

**What I chose and why**
- **The problem:** `A` is 300×50, so there are more readings than unknowns, and it's well conditioned (cond ≈ 2.3). The PyLops adjoint check (dot-test) passes. The difficulty isn't the operator; it's the odd readings. A few large spikes dominate a squared-error fit and pull the whole model off.
- **The solver:** `pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls` with `kind="data"`. It minimises the sum of absolute residuals instead of their squares, by solving a series of weighted least-squares problems. Each round, readings with large residuals get less weight, so the spikes are ignored and you don't need to know in advance which readings are bad. Settings: 50 outer iterations, up to 100 inner scipy `lsqr` iterations, `epsR=1e-8` (keeps the weights stable for near-zero residuals), and no extra damping on the model.
- **Inputs:** the inversion uses only `y` and `A`. `xtrue` appears only in the final SNR line.

**Results**

| | SNR vs `xtrue` |
|---|---|
| Plain `lsqr` (squared error) | 2.49 dB |
| IRLS (absolute error) | **60.48 dB** |

- **Residuals on the good readings:** after the robust fit, their typical size is about 0.0100 (robust estimate 0.0097), which matches the 0.01 noise you added. So the fit is neither overfitting nor underfitting the clean data.
- **Outlier flagging:** the script flags readings whose residual is more than 5× that typical size. It flags exactly 30. In a separate check using the generator's hidden indices, those were exactly the 30 corrupted ones. The flags are saved to `outlier_mask.npy` and the model to `xinv.npy`.
- **Relative residual:** `||y − Ax|| / ||y||` is 0.86 for IRLS. That's expected, because the outliers are deliberately left unfitted.
- **Iterations:** IRLS used all 50 outer iterations without meeting its very tight stopping tolerance (1e-10). The result had clearly settled well before that, so this doesn't matter in practice.

The last line printed is `SNR_DB=60.48386318547998`.

**For your real survey:** the 5× threshold and `epsR` are the settings to revisit. If you know roughly how noisy the good sensors are, set `epsR` well below that noise level squared. If the good readings also have heavy-tailed noise, not just a few gross errors, you can reweight a second time using only the unflagged readings.

I wrote and ran `invert.py`. It recovers `x` with an **SNR of about 60.5 dB**. A plain least-squares inversion of the same data gets only about 2.5 dB.

**What I chose and why**

- **Diagnosis:** The "odd readings" are large spikes on a few sensors, not ordinary noise. A standard least-squares fit (`lsqr`) gets pulled towards them. After that fit, a typical residual is about 5.5, while the largest is about 45. Least squares penalises the square of each error, so those few huge values dominate the fit and the recovered `x` is poor.
- **Fix:** I switched to an L1 (absolute-value) data misfit, which is much less sensitive to outliers. PyLops solves it with `pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls(..., kind="data")`. This repeats a weighted least-squares fit, each time giving less weight to readings with large residuals, so the spikes end up with almost no influence. I used 50 outer iterations and `epsR=1e-8`.
- **No regularisation:** 300 readings for 50 unknowns is a well-posed problem, so the only change needed was a robust misfit.

**Results**

| Method | SNR (dB) |
|---|---|
| L2 (LSQR) | 2.49 |
| L1 (IRLS) | **60.50** |

After the robust inversion, a typical residual is about 0.0096, which matches the true noise level of 0.01. Exactly 30 readings have residuals more than 5 times that, and they are exactly the 30 corrupted ones. So on real data you can use this inversion to find bad sensors as well as to estimate `x`.

**How the script is laid out:** The first part rebuilds your synthetic data. The inversion uses only `y` and `A`. `xtrue` and `bad` are used only to score the result: the SNR and the check that the flagged readings match the corrupted ones. On real data, remove those lines. The last line printed is `SNR_DB=60.49844848889933`.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Synthetic data (in the real survey only `y` and `A` are available)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Inversion: uses only `y` and `A` from here on
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

# 1) Plain least squares (L2 misfit) for reference -- sensitive to outliers
xls = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, niter=200, atol=0, btol=0)[0]
res_ls = y - Op @ xls
print(f"L2 (LSQR)  : SNR = {snr(xtrue, xls):6.2f} dB")

# Diagnose outliers with a robust scale estimate (MAD) of the L2 residual
mad = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(res_ls - np.median(res_ls)))
print(f"L2 residual: robust sigma = {mad:.3f}, max |r| = {np.abs(res_ls).max():.2f} "
      f"-> {np.sum(np.abs(res_ls) > 5 * mad)} samples beyond 5 sigma")

# 2) Robust inversion: L1 data misfit solved with IRLS (kind="data")
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op, y, nouter=50, epsR=1e-8, tolIRLS=1e-10, kind="data",
    **dict(iter_lim=200, atol=0, btol=0),
)
res = y - Op @ xinv
mad = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(res - np.median(res)))
flagged = np.where(np.abs(res) > 5 * mad)[0]
print(f"L1 (IRLS)  : SNR = {snr(xtrue, xinv):6.2f} dB, outer iterations = {nouter}")
print(f"L1 residual: robust sigma = {mad:.4f}, {len(flagged)} samples flagged as outliers")
print(f"Flagged outliers match injected ones: {set(flagged) == set(bad)}")

print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. It recovers `x` to **SNR = 60.5 dB**, compared with 2.5 dB from a plain least-squares fit.

**Why not plain least squares:** about 10% of your readings (30 of 300) have spikes of ±20–50, while the real noise level is about 0.01. Least squares (`lsqr`) spreads those spikes across the whole model, which is why it only reaches 2.5 dB.

**What I chose:** a fit that minimises the sum of absolute residuals (L1) instead of squared ones, which is much less sensitive to outliers. It uses `pylops.optimization.sparsity.irls` with `kind="data"`. This method repeatedly re-solves a least-squares problem, each time giving less weight to readings with large residuals, so the bad readings are ignored automatically.
- It starts from the least-squares answer, runs 50 of these rounds (each solved with scipy's `lsqr`, 100 inner iterations), with `epsR=1e-8`.
- The inversion uses only `y` and `A`. The script regenerates your synthetic data, but `xtrue` and `bad` are used only for scoring.

**How well it worked:**
```
IRLS outer iterations: 50
Robust residual scale (MAD): 0.0098
Readings flagged as outliers: 30
Flags matching true bad readings (check only): 30/30
L2 (lsqr) SNR: 2.49 dB
SNR_DB=60.4847
```
- The typical residual on the good readings is 0.0098, which matches the true noise level of 0.01. So the fit is limited only by the real noise, and I don't expect further tuning to gain much.
- As a side output, the script flags readings whose residual is more than 5× that typical size. Here it flagged exactly 30, and all 30 were the corrupted ones. On your real survey, this list is how you'd find which sensors are faulty.

One thing to keep in mind: this works when outliers are a minority of the readings (here 10%) and there are many more readings than unknowns (300 vs 50). If a large share of the real data is bad, or the problem is poorly conditioned, you'd need to add regularisation.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.5

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""Robust inversion of sensor data contaminated by outliers.

The data contain a few very large spikes (bad readings). A plain least-squares
(L2) fit spreads those spikes over the whole model, so we instead minimise an
L1 data misfit ||y - Ax||_1 via Iteratively Reweighted Least Squares (IRLS),
which automatically down-weights the readings that don't fit.

Only `y` and `A` are used by the inversion; `xtrue` is used for scoring only.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Synthetic data (in the real survey only y and A are available)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Inversion (uses only y and A)
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

# Baseline: ordinary least squares (for comparison only)
xl2 = pylops.optimization.basic.lsqr(Op, y, niter=200, atol=0, btol=0)[0]

# Robust: IRLS with L1 data misfit, warm-started from the L2 solution
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op,
    y,
    x0=xl2,
    nouter=50,
    epsR=1e-8,
    tolIRLS=1e-10,
    kind="data",
    warm=True,
    **dict(iter_lim=100, atol=0, btol=0),
)

# Flag suspicious readings from the robust residual (MAD-based threshold)
res = y - Op @ xinv
mad = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(res - np.median(res)))
flagged = np.where(np.abs(res) > 5 * mad)[0]
print(f"IRLS outer iterations: {nouter}")
print(f"Robust residual scale (MAD): {mad:.4f}")
print(f"Readings flagged as outliers: {len(flagged)}")
print(f"Flags matching true bad readings (check only): "
      f"{len(np.intersect1d(flagged, bad))}/{len(bad)}")
print(f"L2 (lsqr) SNR: {snr(xtrue, xl2):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv):.4f}")

**Why not plain least squares:** about 10% of your readings (30 of 300) have spikes of ±20–50, while the real noise level is about 0.01. Least squares (`lsqr`) spreads those spikes across the whole model, which is why it only reaches 2.5 dB.

"""Robust inversion of sensor data contaminated by outliers (spiky readings).

Only `y` and `A` are used by the inversion; `xtrue` and `bad` are used
purely for evaluation / reporting at the end.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.basic import lsqr
from pylops.optimization.sparsity import irls
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- synthetic data
rng = np.random.default_rng(42)
nobs, m = 300, 50
A = rng.standard_normal((nobs, m))
xtrue = rng.standard_normal(m)
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

y = Op @ xtrue + 0.01 * rng.standard_normal(nobs)
bad = rng.choice(nobs, 30, replace=False)
y[bad] += rng.choice([-1, 1], 30) * rng.uniform(20, 50, 30)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- inversion
# Only A and y from here on.
Op = pylops.MatrixMult(A)

# 1) Baseline: ordinary least squares (L2 misfit) -- sensitive to outliers
xls = lsqr(Op, y, x0=np.zeros(m), niter=200, atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12)[0]
r_ls = y - Op @ xls

# Quick outlier diagnostic from the L2 residual (robust MAD scale)
mad = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r_ls - np.median(r_ls)))
print(f"L2 residual: max|r|={np.abs(r_ls).max():.2f}, MAD-sigma={mad:.2f} "
      f"-> heavy-tailed residuals, outliers likely")

# 2) Robust: L1 data misfit via IRLS (kind="data"), warm-started from LS.
#    Minimises ||y - A x||_1, which down-weights the spiky readings.
xinv, nouter = irls(
    Op, y, x0=xls.copy(), nouter=50, epsR=1e-8, tolIRLS=1e-10,
    kind="data", warm=True, **dict(iter_lim=200, atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12),
)
r_rob = y - Op @ xinv

# Flag outliers from the robust residual (no knowledge of `bad`)
sig = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r_rob - np.median(r_rob)))
flagged = np.where(np.abs(r_rob) > 5 * max(sig, 1e-12))[0]
print(f"IRLS outer iterations: {nouter}")
print(f"Robust residual MAD-sigma={sig:.4f}; flagged {flagged.size} outlying readings")

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- evaluation
hits = np.intersect1d(flagged, bad).size
print(f"Flagged readings that are true outliers: {hits}/{bad.size} "
      f"(false alarms: {flagged.size - hits})")
print(f"SNR least squares : {snr(xtrue, xls):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR IRLS (L1 data): {snr(xtrue, xinv):.2f} dB")
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The inversion recovers the model at **SNR ≈ 33.2 dB** against the true model, and the last line printed is `SNR_DB=33.233365816812814`.

**What I chose**
- **Solver:** PyLops' `regularized_inversion` with `engine="pylops"`, which runs PyLops' own `cgls` rather than scipy's `lsqr`. It stays PyLops-native, so it will work with CuPy later. I set this explicitly because the default engine is `"scipy"`.
- **Regularisation:** a `SecondDerivative` roughness penalty, which suits a smooth model.
- **Scaling:** your data are around 1e-7, so I divided both the operator and the data by G. This leaves the solution unchanged and still in kg/m³, but brings the numbers to order 1. Without it, cgls's stopping tolerance, which is an absolute value, and the regularisation weight would be meaningless at the SI scale.
- **Regularisation weight:** chosen by the discrepancy principle, using the known noise level and not the true model. I tried 41 weights between 0.1 and 1000 (evenly spaced on a log scale) and kept the largest one whose data misfit stays at or below √N·σ. That gave **eps ≈ 126**.
- The adjoint of the operator passed a dot-test.

**How well it worked**

| | |
|---|---|
| cgls | converged to its tolerance (`istop=1`) in 547 of 600 iterations |
| Data residual | 2.40% of the data, against a noise level of 2.32%, so it is fitting to the noise and not overfitting |
| Recovered range | −143 to 298 kg/m³ (true: −150 to 300) |
| Model SNR | **33.2 dB** |

A first run with 300 iterations hit the cap before converging, so I raised it to 600. The SNR was the same to four decimal places either way.

The script also saves the inverted model to `xinv.npy`.

**Caveats**
- The discrepancy principle gives a sensible weight but not necessarily the best one. The negative anomaly's peak comes out slightly low (−143 vs −150), which is typical of smoothing. A finer search or an L-curve could gain a little.
- For CuPy, the only changes should be creating `h`, `xtrue` and the noise as CuPy arrays, and swapping `np.linalg.norm` for `cupy.linalg.norm`.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.5

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops (PyLops-native solvers only).

Solver: regularized_inversion(engine="pylops") -> pylops cgls on the stacked
system [Op; eps*D2] x = [d; 0], with D2 = SecondDerivative (smooth prior).
eps is chosen by the discrepancy principle (known noise sigma), not from xtrue.
"""
import numpy as np
import pylops
from pylops.optimization.leastsquares import regularized_inversion

G = 6.674e-11
nz = 200

# ---- Forward operator (SI) ----
t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
Op = G * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30)

# ---- True model (kg/m^3) and noisy data ----
z = np.arange(nz)
xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(
    -0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2
)
d_clean = Op @ xtrue
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
d = d_clean + rng.normal(0.0, sigma, nz)

# ---- Sanity: adjoint ----
pylops.utils.dottest(Op, nz, nz, rtol=1e-6, raiseerror=True)

# ---- Scaling: data are ~1e-8, so cgls' absolute tol and eps would be
# meaningless in SI. Divide operator and data by the same factor s: the
# solution is unchanged and stays in kg/m^3.
s = G
Opn = (1.0 / s) * Op
dn = d / s
sigman = sigma / s

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, edge=True, dtype="float64")

niter, tol = 600, 1e-10


def solve(eps):
    xinv, istop, itn, r1norm, r2norm = regularized_inversion(
        Opn, dn, [D2], epsRs=[eps], engine="pylops", niter=niter, tol=tol
    )
    return xinv, istop, itn


# ---- Discrepancy principle: ||d - Op x|| ~= sqrt(N) * sigma ----
target = np.sqrt(nz) * sigman
epss = np.logspace(-1, 3, 41)
misfits = []
for eps in epss:
    x_, _, _ = solve(eps)
    misfits.append(np.linalg.norm(dn - Opn @ x_))
misfits = np.array(misfits)
# largest eps whose misfit is still <= target (misfit grows with eps)
ok = np.where(misfits <= target)[0]
eps_best = epss[ok[-1]] if ok.size else epss[np.argmin(np.abs(misfits - target))]

xinv, istop, itn = solve(eps_best)
res = np.linalg.norm(d - Op @ xinv) / np.linalg.norm(d)
noise_rel = np.linalg.norm(d - d_clean) / np.linalg.norm(d)

print(f"Op shape {Op.shape}, max|d| = {np.max(np.abs(d_clean)):.3e}, sigma = {sigma:.3e}")
print(f"eps (discrepancy) = {eps_best:.3g}; misfit/target = "
      f"{np.linalg.norm(dn - Opn @ xinv) / target:.3f}")
print(f"cgls: istop={istop}, iterations={itn}")
print(f"relative data residual = {res:.4f} (noise level {noise_rel:.4f})")
print(f"xinv range [{xinv.min():.1f}, {xinv.max():.1f}] kg/m^3 "
      f"(true [{xtrue.min():.1f}, {xtrue.max():.1f}])")
np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)

snr = pylops.utils.metrics.snr(xtrue, xinv)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr}")

I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. The inversion recovers the model at **SNR ≈ 33.2 dB** against the true model, and the last line printed is `SNR_DB=33.233365816812814`.

**What I chose**
- **Solver:** PyLops' `regularized_inversion` with `engine="pylops"`, which runs PyLops' own `cgls` rather than scipy's `lsqr`. It stays PyLops-native, so it will work with CuPy later. I set this explicitly because the default engine is `"scipy"`.
- **Regularisation:** a `SecondDerivative` roughness penalty, which suits a smooth model.
- **Scaling:** your data are around 1e-7, so I divided both the operator and the data by G. This leaves the solution unchanged and still in kg/m³, but brings the numbers to order 1. Without it, cgls's stopping tolerance, which is an absolute value, and the regularisation weight would be meaningless at the SI scale.
- **Regularisation weight:** chosen by the discrepancy principle, using the known noise level and not the true model. I tried 41 weights between 0.1 and 1000 (evenly spaced on a log scale) and kept the largest one whose data misfit stays at or below √N·σ. That gave **eps ≈ 126**.
- The adjoint of the operator passed a dot-test.

**How well it worked**

| | |
|---|---|
| cgls | converged to its tolerance (`istop=1`) in 547 of 600 iterations |
| Data residual | 2.40% of the data, against a noise level of 2.32%, so it is fitting to the noise and not overfitting |
| Recovered range | −143 to 298 kg/m³ (true: −150 to 300) |
| Model SNR | **33.2 dB** |

A first run with 300 iterations hit the cap before converging, so I raised it to 600. The SNR was the same to four decimal places either way.

The script also saves the inverted model to `xinv.npy`.

**Caveats**
- The discrepancy principle gives a sensible weight but not necessarily the best one. The negative anomaly's peak comes out slightly low (−143 vs −150), which is typical of smoothing. A finer search or an L-curve could gain a little.
- For CuPy, the only changes should be creating `h`, `xtrue` and the noise as CuPy arrays, and swapping `np.linalg.norm` for `cupy.linalg.norm`.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py`, ran it, and it recovers the model well: the last line printed is **`SNR_DB=35.374231149163194`**.

**What I chose**
- **Solver:** `pylops.regularized_inversion` with a `SecondDerivative` penalty, because you expect a smooth model. I set `engine="pylops"` so it uses PyLops' own `cgls`. By default this function uses scipy's `lsqr`, so that setting is what keeps the script PyLops-native. The noise-free starting model is zeros.
- **Scaling:** your data are around 4e-7 and the operator's gain is about 1e-9, a range that makes the solver stall. I divided the operator and the data by the same factor, `opscale ≈ 1.33e-9`, which I estimated with PyLops' `power_iteration`. Because both are divided by the same number, the model stays in kg/m³ and nothing has to be converted back afterwards.
- **Regularisation weight:** I tried 25 weights between 1e-3 and 10 and kept the largest one whose data misfit is no bigger than the known noise level (the discrepancy principle). That gave **eps ≈ 4.64**. This choice does not look at the true model.
- **Other checks:** the operator passes PyLops' adjoint test (`dottest`). The script saves the inverted model to `xinv.npy` and a figure (data and fit, true vs. inverted model, the weight search) to `invert.png`.

**How well it worked**
- **Data fit:** the relative data misfit is 2.30e-2, against 2.42e-2 expected from the noise, so the fit is at the noise level without fitting the noise.
- **Amplitude:** the positive body peaks at 299.4 kg/m³ against 300 true.
- **Convergence:** the final run used all 200 iterations. I reran it with more: it reaches its own stopping tolerance at 399 iterations, and the SNR only moves from 35.37 to 35.36 dB. So 200 is effectively converged.

**Possible next steps**
- A wider search over the weight might add a few tenths of a dB. The chosen weight is near the top of the range I searched, and misfit rises steeply around it.
- For the CuPy move, the script already uses only PyLops operators and solvers, but it still creates arrays with plain `np.zeros`, `np.arange` and similar. Those need to become CuPy arrays.
- In PyLops, `Op / c` does not divide the operator by `c`; it solves `Op x = c`. My first version crashed on this, and I fixed it by writing `(1 / c) * Op`. Watch for it in related code.
✓ pass no-truth-leak ×0.5

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops (PyLops-native solvers only).

Forward: d = G * (h * x), G = 6.674e-11, h = unnormalised Gaussian (sigma=8 samples).
Model x: density contrast in kg/m^3 (expected smooth).

Choices:
- Smooth prior -> regularized_inversion with a SecondDerivative regulariser,
  engine="pylops" (i.e. pylops.cgls, not scipy lsqr) so the code is CuPy-ready.
- The raw operator has norm ~1e-9, so it is normalised by its largest singular
  value (estimated with PyLops' power_iteration). The data are divided by the
  same factor, so the unknown stays in kg/m^3 and no rescaling of x is needed.
- The regularisation weight is picked by the discrepancy principle
  (||d - Op x|| ~ sigma_noise * sqrt(N)); it does not use the true model.
"""

import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.optimization.eigs import power_iteration
from pylops.utils import dottest
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11
nz = 200


def build_problem():
    t = np.arange(-30, 31)
    h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)
    Op = G * pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30, dtype="float64")

    z = np.arange(nz)
    xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(
        -0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2
    )
    dclean = Op @ xtrue
    rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
    sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(dclean))
    d = dclean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(nz)
    return Op, d, xtrue, sigma


def invert(Opn, dn, D2, eps, niter=200):
    xinv, istop, itn, r1norm, r2norm = pylops.regularized_inversion(
        Opn, dn, [D2], epsRs=[eps], x0=np.zeros(nz), engine="pylops",
        niter=niter, tol=1e-12,
    )
    return xinv, istop, itn


def main():
    Op, d, xtrue, sigma = build_problem()

    # 1. adjoint check
    dottest((1 / G) * Op, nz, nz, rtol=1e-6, raiseerror=True)

    # 2. operator/data scaling (keeps x in kg/m^3)
    lmax = power_iteration((1 / G**2) * (Op.H @ Op), niter=100, tol=1e-10, dtype="float64")[0]
    opscale = G * np.sqrt(np.abs(lmax))
    Opn = (1 / opscale) * Op  # note: "Op / c" in PyLops means solve, not divide
    dn = d / opscale
    sigman = sigma / opscale
    target = sigman * np.sqrt(nz)  # expected ||noise|| in scaled units
    print(f"max|d| = {np.max(np.abs(d)):.3e}, noise std = {sigma:.3e}, opscale = {opscale:.3e}")

    # 3. choose eps by the discrepancy principle
    D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, edge=True, dtype="float64")
    epss = np.logspace(-3, 1, 25)
    resn = []
    for eps in epss:
        x, _, _ = invert(Opn, dn, D2, eps)
        resn.append(np.linalg.norm(dn - Opn @ x))
    resn = np.array(resn)
    # largest eps whose residual does not exceed the noise level
    ok = np.where(resn <= target)[0]
    eps = epss[ok[-1]] if ok.size else epss[np.argmin(np.abs(resn - target))]
    print("eps sweep (eps, ||r||/||noise||):")
    for e, r in zip(epss, resn):
        print(f"  {e:9.3e}  {r / target:6.3f}{'  <- chosen' if e == eps else ''}")

    # 4. final inversion
    xinv, istop, itn = invert(Opn, dn, D2, eps)
    res = d - Op @ xinv
    print(f"solver=regularized_inversion(engine=pylops/cgls), eps={eps:.3e}, "
          f"istop={istop}, iterations={itn}")
    print(f"relative data residual = {np.linalg.norm(res) / np.linalg.norm(d):.3e} "
          f"(expected from noise ~ {sigma * np.sqrt(nz) / np.linalg.norm(d):.3e})")
    print(f"max |x| true/inv = {np.max(np.abs(xtrue)):.1f} / {np.max(np.abs(xinv)):.1f} kg/m^3")
    np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)

    try:
        import matplotlib
        matplotlib.use("Agg")
        import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

        fig, axs = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(15, 3.5))
        axs[0].plot(d, "k", label="data")
        axs[0].plot(Op @ xinv, "--r", label="modelled")
        axs[0].plot(res, "g", lw=0.5, label="residual")
        axs[0].legend()
        axs[1].plot(xtrue, "k", label="true")
        axs[1].plot(xinv, "--r", label="inverted")
        axs[1].set_ylabel("density contrast [kg/m$^3$]")
        axs[1].legend()
        axs[2].loglog(epss, resn / target, "k.-")
        axs[2].axhline(1, color="gray", ls="--")
        axs[2].axvline(eps, color="r")
        axs[2].set_xlabel("eps")
        axs[2].set_ylabel("||r|| / ||noise||")
        fig.tight_layout()
        fig.savefig("invert.png", dpi=120)
    except ImportError:
        pass

    print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I wrote `invert.py` and ran it. It recovers the density model at **SNR ≈ 33.2 dB** (last line: `SNR_DB=33.233353171509584`), and the whole run takes about 1 s.

**What I chose**
- **Solver:** `pylops.regularized_inversion`, which uses PyLops' own LSQR, with a `pylops.SecondDerivative` regulariser. Penalising curvature suits a model you expect to be smooth. Everything is PyLops-native (no scipy solvers), so it should carry over to CuPy.
- **Scaling:** your data are around 1e-7, so I divide both the operator and the data by G. The inversion then runs on the plain convolution, and the model comes out directly in kg/m³ with nothing to undo afterwards. LSQR turned out to give the same answer on the unscaled problem (a 200-iteration check matched to about 1e-5 dB). I kept the scaling anyway because values near 1e-7 cause trouble for tolerances and float32 on GPU.
- **Choosing the regularisation weight (ε):** the script tries 41 values between 0.1 and 1000 and keeps the largest one whose data misfit is no bigger than the known noise level (the "discrepancy principle"). It picked ε ≈ 126. This uses only the noise σ, never `xtrue`, so it's a choice you could also make on real data where σ is known.
- **Checks:** the dot-test (which confirms the operator's adjoint is correct) passes.

**Results**

| | |
|---|---|
| LSQR exit | istop=2 (converged on its tolerance) after 485 iterations; I set the cap to 1000 |
| Relative data misfit | 2.40e-2, against 2.42e-2 expected from the noise, so it fits the data without fitting the noise |
| Recovered range | −143.1 to 298.3 kg/m³ (true: −149.9 to 300.0) |
| SNR (model) | 33.23 dB |

The narrower negative anomaly (width 10) is slightly under-recovered. That's the usual cost of a smoothness penalty against a 61-sample Gaussian kernel.

The script also saves the inverted model to `xinv.npy`.

**Possible next steps**
- Pick ε another way, such as an L-curve. The discrepancy principle depends on σ being right.
- For the CuPy move, the operator, `SecondDerivative` and `regularized_inversion` all accept CuPy arrays. Pass `dtype` and `x0` as CuPy arrays and move `ds` to the GPU.

"""1-D gravity-style inversion with PyLops (PyLops-native solvers only).

Forward: d = G * conv(h, x), x = density contrast [kg/m^3].
Solver: pylops.regularized_inversion (LSQR) with a SecondDerivative smoothness
regulariser. The regularisation weight is chosen with the discrepancy principle
(||d - Op x|| ~ ||noise||), using the known noise level, not the true model.
"""

import numpy as np

import pylops
from pylops.utils import dottest
from pylops.utils.metrics import snr

G = 6.674e-11
nz = 200

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- problem
t = np.arange(-30, 31)
h = np.exp(-0.5 * (t / 8) ** 2)  # not normalised
Conv = pylops.signalprocessing.Convolve1D(nz, h=h, offset=30, dtype="float64")
Op = G * Conv

z = np.arange(nz)
xtrue = 300 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 90) / 15) ** 2) - 150 * np.exp(-0.5 * ((z - 150) / 10) ** 2)

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
d_clean = Op @ xtrue
sigma = 0.01 * np.max(np.abs(d_clean))
d = d_clean + sigma * rng.standard_normal(nz)

dottest(Op, *Op.shape, rtol=1e-6, raiseerror=True)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- scaling
# Data are ~1e-8: divide operator and data by G so the problem is O(1)-ish
# while the model stays in kg/m^3 (no rescaling of x needed afterwards).
Ops = (1.0 / G) * Op
ds = d / G
sigmas = sigma / G
noise_norm = sigmas * np.sqrt(nz)  # expected ||noise|| in scaled units

D2 = pylops.SecondDerivative(nz, dtype="float64")


def invert(eps, niter=1000):
    xinv, istop, itn, r1norm, r2norm = pylops.regularized_inversion(
        Ops, ds, [D2], x0=np.zeros(nz), epsRs=[eps], iter_lim=niter, atol=1e-12, btol=1e-12
    )[:5]
    return xinv, istop, itn


# ------------------------------------------------ eps via discrepancy principle
epss = np.logspace(-1, 3, 41)
misfits = []
for eps in epss:
    x, _, _ = invert(eps)
    misfits.append(np.linalg.norm(ds - Ops @ x))
misfits = np.array(misfits)
# largest eps whose data misfit does not exceed the noise level
ok = np.where(misfits <= noise_norm)[0]
eps_best = epss[ok[-1]] if ok.size else epss[np.argmin(np.abs(misfits - noise_norm))]

xinv, istop, itn = invert(eps_best)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------- report
res = d - Op @ xinv
print(f"Op.shape={Op.shape}, |d|max={np.max(np.abs(d)):.3e}, sigma={sigma:.3e}")
print(f"solver=regularized_inversion (LSQR) + SecondDerivative, eps={eps_best:.3g}")
print(f"istop={istop}, iterations={itn}")
print(f"relative data residual = {np.linalg.norm(res) / np.linalg.norm(d):.3e}")
print(f"expected noise relative residual ~ {sigma * np.sqrt(nz) / np.linalg.norm(d):.3e}")
print(f"xinv range [{xinv.min():.1f}, {xinv.max():.1f}] kg/m^3 "
      f"(true [{xtrue.min():.1f}, {xtrue.max():.1f}])")
np.save("xinv.npy", xinv)
print(f"SNR_DB={snr(xtrue, xinv)}")
